  • ● The LCM process in Northern Ireland (involving the agreement of the Northern Ireland Executive,therelevantcommitteescrutinyandvoteoftheNorthernIrelandAssemblyitself) can generally take around eight weeks. ThereisnourgentprocedureintheAssembly’s StandingOrders,althoughStandingOrderscanbesuspendedwiththeagreementofthe Northern Ireland Assembly itself.

APPENDIX E: DELEGATED LEGISLATION TOOLKIT

  • ● An Act of Parliament can give ministers or others thepower(sometimescalleda ‘delegatedpower’)tomakesecondarylegislation. Thistoolkitwillhelpyoudecide whethertoincludedelegatedpowersinabill.Itsetsoutsomeguidingprinciplesand practical examples (which are not intended to be exhaustive).
  • ● The toolkit covers the following issues:

○ What is the right balance between primary and secondary legislation?

○ What is the proper role for delegated powers?

○ Where will delegated powers not generally be justifiable?

○ In what circumstances may it be appropriate to take powers that enable primary legislation to be amended (so called “Henry VIII powers”)?

○ What alternatives should be considered when deciding whether to take a delegated power?

○ What safeguardsshouldbeconsideredwhenithasbeendecidedtotakea delegated power?

  • ● Questions about the application of this guidance should be discussed in the first instancewithdepartmentalpolicyofficialsandlawyers,whocanliaisewiththeOffice of the Parliamentary Counsel and, as appropriate, with the Attorney General’s Office/Legal Secretariat to the Advocate General for Scotland for clarification or further assistance.
  • ● Theseissueswillultimatelyneedtoberesolvedaspartoftheusualprocessesfor agreeingtheformandcontentoflegislationbeforeintroduction,andwithministerial discussion where required.
  • ● This guidance is exclusively concerned with decisions aboutwhethertotakenew powersinprimarylegislation.Itisnotintendedtodealwiththeexerciseofexisting powers to make secondary legislation.

A. What is the right balance between primary and secondarylegislation?

  • ● An Act of Parliament is the way that the most important aspects of policy are translated into law. An Act should contain the main aspects of a policy at an appropriate level of detail to be meaningfullyscrutinisedbyParliament,andtobe understood and applied by those at whom it is aimed.
  • ● Delegated powers are generallyusedtogiveeffecttothepolicyframeworkinthe Act.

Example

  • ● TheMarriage (Same Sex Couples) Act 2013introducedmarriage for same-sex couples in England and Wales. The Act allowed existing civil partnerships to be converted into a marriage. A delegated power was taken to allow ministers to set out how that procedure should work in practice (e.g. the information that should be provided to support an application).
  • ● Asaruleofthumb,themoresignificantalegalchange,thestrongerthepresumption thatitshouldbesetoutinprimarylegislationsothatitcanbedebatedbyParliament via the bill process, rather than included within the scope of a delegated power.

B. What is the proper role for delegated powers?

  • ● Secondary legislation is an indispensablepartofmodernlawmaking,butitisvital thatitisusedappropriately,andnotabused.Thescopeofpowers,therationalefor theiruse,andwhetheranysafeguardsareappropriate(asbelow)shouldbeproperly tested and justified in every case.
  • ● Delegated powers are more likely to be appropriate for matters such as:

○ dealingwithsecondarypolicydetailrequiredtogiveeffecttotheAct,including technical or administrative matters (e.g. application processes, detailed procedural rules);

○ making consequential changes to other legislation;

○ includingdetailwhichmayneedamendingmoreoftenthanParliamentcanbe expected to legislate for by primary legislation, incircumstanceswherethe purpose of the Act will be defeated if changes could only be achieved by furtherbills(e.g.inanareaofrapidlyevolvingtechnologywherereferencesto technical concepts could quickly become defunct);

○ dealingwiththingswhichcanreasonablybeanticipatedtochangeregularlyin the future (e.g. uprating sums in line with inflation);

○ accommodating the fact that a detailed policy has to work differently for differentgroupsofpeople,differentareas,etc.(e.g.tosetupapilotscheme for a new policy in one part of the country) or has to deal with niche cases;

○ dealingwithemergencysituations,whereprimarylegislationisnotfeasibleas a means of responding to fast-moving events (e.g. public health emergencies).

C. Where will delegated powers not generally be justifiable?

  • ● Delegatedpowersshouldnotgenerallybetakensoastomakeprovisioninfuture whichiscentraltotheeffectoftheBill.Thatdetailshouldnormallybeincludedon the face of a bill for scrutiny and debate as part of its passage.
  • ● For example, delegated powers should only exceptionally be taken to deal with:

○ thecreationorextensionofgenerallyapplicablecriminaloffencesorpenalties (andregulatoryoffences,unlesstheyaretobecreatedbypowerscontaining appropriate safeguards – see section F below);

○ measures effecting significant constitutional change.

  • ● Itisofparticularimportancethatadelegatedpowerwhichiscapableofinterfering with human rights is appropriately justifiedandcontainspropersafeguards(which may vary according to the nature oftherightandthesignificanceofthepotential interference).
  • ● Delegated powers are unlikely to be appropriate, for example:

○ tolegislateforessentialaspectsofapolicyframeworkwhichhavenotbeen setoutintheAct(including‘skeletonclauses’whichleavetherealsubstance of the Act to regulations made under it);

○ because there has been insufficient time for proper policy development; or where powers are being included speculatively on a ‘just in case’ basis.

D. In what circumstances may it be appropriate to take Henry VIII powers?

  • ● Asageneralrule,anActofParliamentshouldbeamendedorrepealedbyanother Act. Powers to amend or modify primary legislation by secondary legislation (sometimes called‘HenryVIII’powers)shouldonlybetakenwheretheirnecessity has been rigorously tested, and appropriate safeguards – or alternatives – fully considered.
  • ● Henry VIII powers are sometimes misunderstood as being unacceptable in all circumstances. What really matters, though, is the scope of the power, and the substance of the change that is capable of being made to primary legislation.
  • ● Some examples of where Henry VIII powers may be acceptable include:

○ toamenddetailwhichcouldwellhavebeenlefttosecondarylegislation(see sectionB)buthasbeenincludedinprimarylegislationtomakethelaweasier for the reader to understand;

○ tomakeconsequentialamendmentstolegislationinlightofchangesmadeby an Act;

○ toaddnewcasestoalegalframeworksetoutinanAct,wherenotbeingable todosowouldunderminethepolicyofthatAct(e.g.inapolicyenvironment involvingrapidtechnologicalorotherchange,orwherethereislikelihoodof abuse or avoidance).

Section 339ZH(10) of theProceeds of Crime Act 2002allows the Secretary of State to uprate in line with inflation the maximum penalty set out in the Act for failing to comply with a court order

Example 2

Section 10 of theGenetic Technology (Precision Breeding)Act 2023allows the definition of 'relevant animal' in the Act to be updated in order to ensure consistency with related regulatory regimes.

  • ● By contrast, Henry VIII powers should not generally (and not without appropriate safeguards) be taken to amend the matters that are identified above as only exceptionally being the proper subject of a delegated power (see section C above).
  • ● ThestartingassumptionisthatHenryVIIIpowersshouldbesubjecttotheaffirmative procedure unless, having regard to the substance of the power, there is a good justificationforadifferentlevelofscrutiny(e.g.becausethepowerisonlycapableof making amendments of a minor character).

E. What alternatives should be considered when deciding whether to take a delegated power?

  • ● Some examples of alternative approaches to taking a delegated power may be:

○ delaying introduction of the Bill until policy formulation is more advanced;

○ including, on the face of the Bill, more aspects of the policy that a power seeks to implement;

○ where policy formulation is still ongoing, to consider whether it may be possible and appropriate to proceed by way of Government amendment at a later stage.

F. What safeguards should be considered when it has been decided to take a delegated power?

  • ● Powersshouldbenowiderthannecessarytomeettheirintendedobjective.Thisis particularly important where the power is capable of amending primary legislation.
  • ● Thewiderapower,themorelikelyitisthatspecificsafeguardsshouldbeincludedto upholdtheproperconstitutionalbalancebetweenParliamentandtheexecutive,and to guard against future abuse.
  • ● Some examples of appropriate safeguards, or alternative approaches, may include:

○ expressrestrictionsonthescopeofapower(e.g.limitingtotheexpansion, rather than removal of rights granted by anAct;limitingitsapplicationtoa particular group of people/typeofcase;settingstatutorymaximumlimitson matters such as fines and sentences);

○ a test or threshold that must be met, or process undertaken (e.g. consultation), before a power can be exercised;

○ cleardescriptionof,orparametersaround,whatthepowercanpositivelydo (e.g.describingastightlyaspossiblethekindofconductthatmayamountto a regulatory offence);

○ appropriate parliamentary scrutiny procedures: as a rule of thumb, powers capable of making more significant provision should be subject to the affirmative resolution procedure;

○ sunsetprovisions(whichmeanthatthepowerwillexpire,ormustberenewed by Parliament, after a given period of time).

  • ● The implications of these different approaches will need to be carefully worked through, including by reference to legal and delivery risks.

APPENDIX G: CHECKLISTS FOR EACH STAGE

Thesechecklistsarenotcomprehensiveandarenottobereadinisolationbutreferred to alongside the relevant sections of the Guide. Youshouldreadallofthechapterson parliamentary stages in the Guide first to allow for timely preparation in advance of deadlines.

PREPARING A BILL FOR INTRODUCTION

Before drafting legislation

Bid for the bill, completing a bid template provided by the PBL Secretariat. Contact the PBL Secretariat to discuss the bid. Obtain drafting authority from the Leader of the House of Commons for OPC to draft the bill. Put in place resources: a bill team, legal team, and governance mechanisms. If there is a joint bill team with two or more departments, agree a protocol for managing the bill. Produce a clear delivery plan for developing the bill and supporting documents from now until after Royal Assent. Arrange for training (contact the Parliamentary Capability team or the PBL Secretariat). Engage the Union and Devolution Directorate, Territorial Offices and then the devolved governments on any devolved implications of the bill.

During drafting

Obtain collective agreement for the policy in the bill. Work with departments across-government on any implications for their areas. Contact the AGO and LSAG to discuss the key legal issues in the bill and whether any Law Officer consents will be required.

Consider the duty on Ministers of the Crown in section 19 of the Environment Act 2021 to have due regard to the environmental principles policy statement when making policy, including proposals that lead to legislation. Contact the United Kingdom Intelligence Community to discuss whether the proposals impact on work of the Agencies. Consider the impact, if any, on the Crown Dependencies and British overseas territories, as necessary, in consultation with MoJ and FCDO. Contact the Data Protection Policy Team in DSIT to discuss whether the proposals which relate to the processing of personal data are compatible with data protection legislation. Consult the Information Commissioner's Office on any new data sharing proposals or gateways Address any finance, public expenditure or tax implications of the bill and obtain the agreement of HM Treasury where necessary. Seek Cabinet Office and HM Treasury ministerial approval before taking steps to establish, disestablish, re-classify, or merge an Arm’s-Length Body (ALB), if applicable. Give the House Authorities an opportunity to comment on proposals intended to apply in relation to Parliament or the parliamentary estate. Begin work on your economic and equalities impact assessments, if applicable. Begin work on explanatory notes, delegated powers memorandum, ECHR memorandum, factsheets, “keeling schedules” and other supporting documents. Keep in regular contact with the PBL Secretariat.

Before PBL Committee consideration of the bill for introduction

Ensure you have a plan to draft and deliver the documents required, and assigned members of the team. You will need to provide (see below for detailed checklists on each product): Bill print (OPC will provide) Explanatory Notes PBL Memorandum Parliamentary handling strategy Legal Issues Memorandum Delegated Powers Memorandum, if needed Impact Assessment and RPC opinion, if needed Ask the PBL Secretariat for the templates. Documents need to be circulated to PBL Secretariat one week before the Committee meeting, with drafts shared the week before that, or in the case of the Legal Issues Memo, at least three weeks before. Ensure the AGO and LSAG are sent the draft legal issues memorandum three weeks before documents are sent to the PBL Committee. AGO and LSAG officials will need to comment on any draft requests for retrospection or early commencement in good time for the final requests to be sent to AGO and LSAG.

Explanatory Notes:

Identify the personnel who will be responsible for preparing the explanatory notes (ENs), including the person who will act as the point of contact with the Public Bill Offices (PBO). Begin drafting the ENs (using the template), starting with the sections on policy background and legal background. Seek comments from departmental lawyers throughout drafting of the Bill, including the final version of the ENs before they are sent to PBL Committee. Consider whether you should consult OPC on parts of the ENs relating to territorial extent and application. If the bill is to be introduced in the House of Commons, ask OPC to provide, or to clear, the wording for the part of the ENs that relate to parliamentary approval for financial costs or charges imposed. Consult the Territorial Offices and Office of the Advocate General (as appropriate) to provide final clearance for the relevant sections of the notes. Ensure the final draft of the ENs are consistent with the final text of the bill. Arrange for the Public Bill Office to comment on the explanatory notes (at least two days before introduction). Action any comments from the PBO.

Prepare a PBL Committee memorandum

Draft the Memo, summarising the content of the bill and any other important issues which have been covered in the other PBL Committee documents referenced above.

Proof read the final version of the Memo, ensuring all sections are complete.

Prepare a final parliamentary handling strategy

Draft the handling strategy. Share the draft handling strategy with the Whips at least one week before they are submitted to the PBL Committee for circulation. Action any comments to from the Government Whips’ Offices in the Commons and Lords.

Produce a delegated powers memorandum (DPM), if applicable,for publication Draft the DPM, explaining the purpose of and justification for any delegation of power in the bill Share the DPM with Lords Whips’ Office at least one week before they are submitted to the PBL Committee for circulation. Action any comments from the Government Whips’ Office in the Lords.

Produce full impact assessment, if applicable

Engage with your departmental Better Regulation team. Produce the impact assessment. Engage with the RPC, where appropriate. Action any comments from the RPC in relation to an initial opinion.

Draft the Legal Issues Memorandum (LIM)

Draft the LIM, setting out the justification for any provisions that may potentially be considered to touch on Convention rights in a legal issues memorandum. Consider the bill's compatibility with the European Convention on Human Rights and arrange for the bill minister to sign the ECHR statement to support the bill. Consider whether the bill contains environmental law (NB: the minister may need to sign a statement, or include a sentence in the ENs). Consider whether the bill relates to Northern Ireland-Great Britain trade (NB: the minister may need to sign a statement, or include a sentence in the ENs) Consider whether the bill is compatible with Scottish and Northern Ireland law. Consult with the Territorial Offices and Office of the Advocate General as appropriate in relation to any devolution matters.

Before PBL Committee

Clear the PBL Committee documents with your minister/s. Submit the papers to PBL Secretariat one week before the PBL Committee meeting. Brief your minister ahead of the PBL Committee meeting.

THE HOUSE OF COMMONS

Ahead of presentation/First Reading in the Commons

Arrange bill backers If applicable, arrange for the minister to sign an ECHR statement. Arrange for the minister to sign an environment law statement, or include a sentence in the ENs Arrange for the minister to sign a Northern Ireland-Great Britain trade statement, or include a sentence in the ENs. Agree that OPC can give notice of presentation of the bill. Send the final bill print and ENs to the PBO (OPC will send the bill print; the bill team should send other documents including the ENs). The PBO will add the bill number and arrange printing of both documents. Confirm with the Whips Office which minister will sit on the front bench to 'nod' in the bill. If necessary, write to the devolved governments requesting their agreement to initiate the legislative consent process. You should repeat this whenever the bill is amended in a way which engages the legislative consent process.

On the day of introduction

Send the delegated powers memorandum to Commons PBO for publication (requires sending to the DPRRC when the Bill reaches the Lords - see Lords intro requirements). Share soft copies with the PBO of: the impact assessment and Regulatory Policy Committee opinion (if applicable), ECHR memo (if separate analysis has been undertaken). Send the ECHR memo to the JCHR. Provide printed copies of relevant documents to the PBO, including:

50 copies of the impact assessment and Regulatory Policy Committee opinion (if applicable).

Arrange for publication on GOV.UK (on dedicated Bill page) of:

Impact assessment(s) and Regulatory Policy Committee opinion (if applicable), Delegated powers memo and ECHR memo (if relevant), and Other supporting statements (e.g. fact sheets) where relevant. Arrange for deposit of supporting documents in the House libraries.

After the bill has been introduced

Arrange for a written ministerial statement (WMS) to be issued (if relevant) and agreed with the Whips offices and No.10. Send a 'Dear Colleagues' letter to MPs (as agreed with the Commons Whips office and No.10). Send stakeholder and select committee correspondence (where relevant). Issue a press notice and media briefings (where relevant).

Ahead of Commons Second Reading

Keep in touch with the Whips about the timing for Second Reading. Meet the minister to understand their briefing requirements. Arrange for ministers to open and close - agree the approach with the Whips Office. Draft an opening speech, including details of the bill. Draft a skeleton closing speech, with placeholders to respond to the expected areas of questioning, and rebuttals to any reasoned amendments tabled. Provide a Q&A pack with top lines to respond to anticipated issues that will come up during the debate. Take stock of stakeholder briefings and wider public narratives ahead of debate - and reflect positions in the briefing pack. Ensure you have copies of speech for Hansard, and copies of the final briefing pack that can be annotated and passed to the minister (via the PPS) as necessary. Agree officials to attend the box with the departmental parliamentary branch (establish a virtual box for policy leads/lawyers).

After Commons Second Reading

Provide a readout of the debate across the department, PBL Secretariat and business managers. Follow up on any commitments e.g. 'I will write' letters.

Commons Committee Stage

Provide the Whips with a list of witnesses for oral evidence sessions (agreed by the bill minister). With Whips and OPC, prepare programming resolution and standard motions for sitting in private and publication of written evidence. Brief minister for programming sub-committee. Prepare suggested questions for the minister to ask witnesses. Develop a tracker for amendments. Agree a process for responding to amendments and briefing ministers with policy, legal, press and private offices (including drafting / clearance processes). Communicate deadlines and expected pressure points to the department. Arrange an early discussion with the Committee Clerk and agree process for the bill team to comment on draft groupings. Consider providing a procedural brief on how Committee works to the bill minister (in discussion with the Whips office). Draft clause/schedule stand part notes for each clause/schedule in the bill. Agree suggested Government amendments needed with the PBL Secretariat and Whips offices before seeking collective agreement. Seek collective agreement (PBL Committee and policy committee if needed) Factor in time for instructing OPC to prepare any Government amendments. Commission notes on amendments (explanations of amendments, speaking note and Q&A) to be cleared by legal and (if necessary) communications colleagues. Stay in touch with the Whips offices about timing for Committee and what will be debated when. Table Government amendments at least a week before consideration (having agreed that they can be tabled with the Whips); provide details in a letter to co-chairs of the Public Bill Committee (enclosing, as appropriate, supplementary delegated powers and ECHR memos).

If needed, discuss final grouping with the Clerk directly, or via OPC. Provide final ministerial briefing pack. Brief minister/s Agree officials to attend the box and alert the departmental parliamentary branch. Establish a virtual box as required for those not attending in person. During Committee:keep a list of Ministerial commitmentsand provide a readout of the debate across the department, PBL Secretariat and business managers. Prepare script for minister at end of committee thanking co-chairs etc. Ensure your explanatory notes are updated and ready to be re-published on Lords introduction If applicable, ensure your Delegated Powers Memo is updated and ready to be published as soon as Committee Stage has concluded. Keep in regular contact with the PBL Secretariat.

Commons Remaining stages (Report and Third Reading)

Keep in touch with the Whips office about the timing of remaining stages, including tabling deadlines and how the debate will run. Obtain clearance for any Government amendments, factoring in time to instruct OPC to prepare any Government amendments that are wanted. Send a letter detailing amendments to the Official Opposition spokesperson (enclosing supplementary ECHR and delegated powers memos where appropriate). Table amendments at least one week before the debate (having agreed that they can be tabled with the Whips). Send a letter detailing amendments to the Official Opposition spokesperson (enclosing supplementary ECHR and delegated powers memos, where appropriate). If necessary, issue further Dear Colleague letter covering key Government amendment and high-profile non-government amendments. If you are at the final amending stage in the second House, ensure that any LCMs have been passed by the devolved legislatures. Commission notes on amendments (explanations of amendments, speaking note and Q&A) to be cleared by legal and (if necessary) communications colleagues. Agree with Whips terms of any supplementary programme motion. If needed, discuss final grouping with the Clerk directly, or via OPC.

Provide a Q&A pack with top lines to respond to anticipated issues that could come up during the debate. Brief minister. Arrange any briefing sessions with MPs that may be required. If the bill requires King’s and/or Prince's consent, check that the Order Paper mentions this; if this has been omitted, it should be pointed out to OPC who will alert the House Authorities. Arrange for a Minister, who is a privy counsellor, to signify Consent just before Third Reading, if applicable. Ensure the Minister has a short Third Reading speech setting out how the bill has changed since introduction and thank Members for their interest in the bill. Provide a readout of the debate across the department, PBL Secretariat and business managers.

Preparing to transfer to the second House

Ensure the ‘take up’ arrangements are clear (i.e. which Minister is to take charge of the bill in the second House). Agree an approach to parliamentary engagement with the Whips Offices. Ensure the bill, as amended in the Commons, is proof read and approved for publication. Ensure explanatory notes are revised as necessary to publish alongside the bill. These needs to be cleared with the Public Bill Office in the Lords at least two to three working days before the bill is sent to the Lords. Arrange a fresh ECHR statement to be signed. If the Bill contains environmental law, arrange for the Lords Minister in charge of the Bill to sign an environmental law statement. If the bill relates to Northern Ireland-Great Britain trade, arrange for the Lords Minister in charge of the Bill to sign a statement, or include a sentence in the ENs. Revise and arrange for re-publication of the impact assessment(s) and delegated powers memorandum as necessary,

THE HOUSE OF LORDS

Lords Introduction and First Reading

Note that after Third Reading in the Commons, the House copy of the print incorporating amendments (signed by the Clerk of the House) is delivered to the House of Lords with a message. There is no formal process for the Lords Minister to move First Reading (this will be done by the Whips.

If the bill starts in the Lords:

Agree that OPC can alert the PBO to the intention to introduce a bill (different to notice of presentation). Ensure the government Peer is lined up to read the long title. If the bill started in the Commons, theWhip will read out the short title andformally move First Reading. Send draft explanatory notes to the PBO for comment (at least two to three working days before introduction). Arrange for the Lords Minister in charge of the Bill to sign an ECHR statement. If the bill contains environmental law, arrange for the Lords Minister in charge of the Bill to sign an environmental law statement. If the bill relates to Northern Ireland-Great Britain trade, arrange for the Lords Minister in charge of the bill to sign a statement, or include a sentence in the ENs. Send the delegated powers memorandum to the DPRRC Committee.

Whether the bill starts in the Lords or Commons, departments also will need to:

Send final explanatory notes to the PBO the day before publication. Share soft copies of the impact assessment(s), and ECHR memo (if separate analysis has been undertaken) with the Lords Publishing Team for publication on the Bill’s Parliament webpage.

Arrange for publication on GOV.UK (on dedicated Bill page) of:

Impact assessment(s) and Regulatory Policy Committee opinion (if applicable), Delegated powers memo and ECHR memo (if relevant), and Other supporting statements (e.g. fact sheets) where relevant.

Make provision for revised editions of Acts ("keeling schedules") (where necessary).

Arrange press briefings and memos to be publishedafterpublication of the bill and

supporting documents.

Set up necessary engagement with peers, either through an all-peers meeting or

attending party group meetings.

Send any All Peers letters (as agreed with the Lords Whips office and No.10).

Lords Second Reading

Ahead of Second Reading, agree and arrange briefings for the lead Minister - usually the same Minister opens and closes. Draft an opening speech. Draft a skeleton closing speech and a briefing pack providing lines on issues that might come up. Keep track of the Bill speakers list onthe GovernmentWhips’ Official websitebefore the debate. The final list can be obtained from the Government Whips’ Office around lunchtime on the day of Second Reading (10am on Thursday). Agree and arrange for officials to sit in the box including provision of passes. During debate, provide any additional responses needed for the closing speech via notes to the Minister. After the debate, respond to any outstanding questions or points via correspondence within 24 hours. Update your parliamentary handling strategy based on what was raised during debate.

Lords Committee Stage

Stay in touch with the Whips offices about timing and what will be debated and when. Consider providing a procedural brief on how Committee works to the bill Minister (in discussion with the Whips offices). Design a system for tracking amendments and applying dummy numbers to ensure consistency. Ensure any necessary Government amendments have been drafted, collectively agreed and tabled (having agreed that they can be tabled with the Whips) at least one week before Committee. Factor in time to instruct OPC to prepare these. Agree handling and communication of amendments being tabled with the Lords Whips office. Send any supplementary delegated powers memo to DPRRC. Agree a process for responding to amendments and briefing ministers with policy, legal, press and private offices (including drafting / clearance processes). Communicate deadlines and expected pressure points to the department. Draft clause/Schedule stand part notes for each clause/Schedule in the bill. Commission notes on amendments (explanations of amendments, speaking note and Q&A) to be cleared by legal and (if necessary) communications colleagues. Provide the Whips office suggested groupings for debate. Finalise the ministerial pack based on final groupings. Brief the minister. If needed, ensure your explanatory notes are updated as soon as Committee Stage has concluded, though republication at this stage is highly unusual (see 10.30).

Lords Report

Keep in touch with the Whips office about the timing, including tabling deadlines and how the debate will run. Obtain clearance for any Government amendments or concessions in the usual way, factoring in time for OPC to prepare these and table before the deadline (having agreed that they can be tabled with the Whips).Agreehandling and communication of amendments being tabled with the Lords Whips office. Send any supplementary delegated powers memo to DPRRC. Keep PBL Secretariat, No.10 and the Whips Offices regularly updated on risk areas (e.g. where there may be votes / defeats) and ensure there are processes for agreeing the approach to each amendment. Draft notes on amendments to agreed drafting / clearance process. Agree an approach to ministerial briefing and clearance of the approach. If you are at the final amending stage in the second House, ensure that any LCMs have been passed by the devolved legislatures. Respond to DPRRC, Constitution Committee and JCHR, if applicable. Provide the Whips office a suggested grouping of amendments and update the Minister’s pack based on the final groupings (noting that Peers can change groupings on the day of debate).

Brief the minister. If applicable, ensure your explanatory notes are updated and ready to be published as soon as Report Stage has concluded.

Lords Third Reading

Keep in touch with the Whips office about the timing, including tabling deadlines and how the debate will run. Draft notes on any amendments tabled (note that theonly amendments that are admissible are tidying up amendments and amendments to enable the Government to fulfil undertakings given at an earlier stage). On tabling any Government amendments, send a letter to the Official Opposition spokespersons (attaching, as necessary, supplementary delegated powers and/or ECHR memos). Send any supplementary delegated powers memo to DPRRC. Ensure the Minister has a short speech for 'Bill Do Now Pass'. It is customary to thank Members for their interest in the bill. Prepare a statement for the ministertoreadbeforeThirdReadingcommencesto informtheHouseofthebill’spresentLCMstatuswhereconsenthasnotyetbeen received or has not been granted. Brief the minister. Ensure your explanatory notes are updated and ready to be published following Royal Assent.

If the bill started in the Lords, see:Preparing totransfer to the second Houseabove for the checklist.

Ping-pong

Note: there are various complexities to ping-pong that cannot be captured in a checklist. This is therefore designed to give a sense of the issues to consider, but further detailed planning and training should be undertaken ahead of this stage.

Keep in touch with both Whips offices about the timing, including tabling deadlines and how the debate will run. Arrange training for the bill team (the PBL Secretariat in consultation with OPC and the Whips Offices can support this).

Complete early analysis of issues that could be in play at ping-pong with options for responding. Agree the approach to issues outlined above with the PBL Secretariat, No.10 and the Whips offices, and where necessary seeking contingent collective agreement from PBL Committee and the relevant policy committee to a range of options. This will enable speedy deployment if needed. If necessary, ahead of Commons Consideration of Lords Amendments, issue further Dear Colleague letter covering key Government Lords amendments and non-government amendments. Engage both MPs and peers ahead of ping-pong to ensure strong intelligence on the options and approach the House is likely to take (agree your strategy with the Whips Offices). Agree the approach to all amendments and ensure OPC have the necessary instructions to draft the motions for debate, including reasons for seeking to overturn any amendments. Prepare briefing for the Minister to respond to amendments during debate. Ask the PBL Secretariat for the explanatory notes template used during ping-pong. On return to the first House for the first time (only), submit explanatory notes on any amendments (government and non-government) made in the second House to the PBO. In the Commons, it is for the bill minister to propose groupings for Lords amendments for debate. This should be done in consultation with the Whips Office. If an amendment is overturned / rejected in the Commons, a Reasons Committee will meet to provide a reason for this. This is arranged by the Whips office, but the bill team will be asked to: Input the reasons drafted by OPC; and Confirm the name of the minister serving on the Committee. Keep the master explanatory notes updated throughout ping pong as these will be needed once the bill reaches Royal Assent.

Royal Assent

Ensure the explanatory notes are updated to reflect any changes to the bill throughout the bill's passage through the second House and during ping-pong..

They need to be in line with the Guide to Making Legislation (Note: no new template is needed; it is the original template with elements removed as set out in the Guide). Ensure the Act and explanatory notes are proof-read before publication. Send the final explanatory notes to Legislation Services at The National Archives for formatting and publication alongside the text of the Act. Formally handover any work to the teams working on implementation e.g. any secondary legislation, guidance needed. Departments should highlight any debates that were pertinent to ongoing work to ensure this is factored into future handling Conduct a lessons learned exercise (the PBL Secretariat or the Parliamentary Capability can support this). Ensure all templates, documents and submissions are saved in shared areas for archive. Share best practice and consider training for wider bill teams or within the department. Arrange a celebration.

PRIVATE MEMBERS' BILLS

Ensure the department has a good system for tracking bills introduced by Members and for agreeing a position. Ensure you are engaging with your legislative strategy team and/or parliamentary branch.

Taking a position on PMBs that have been introduced

Determine the policy and legislative implications, including the impact on the devolved governments, legal issues, compatibility with the European Convention of Human Rights, regulatory impact, and spending implications. Ensure a clear ministerial decision is taken on whether to: Support the bill; Oppose the bill; or Remain neutral If the Minister wishes to support the bill or take a neutral position, collective agreement must be sought. Engage with the PBL Secretariat as soon as possible to ask for bill document templates.

Attach to the write round: PBL memorandum Bill print Explanatory notes Legal issues memorandum (LIM) Parliamentary handling strategy, Delegated powers memo (if applicable) Impact assessment (if applicable). Engage with OPC on drafting the bill print. Contact the AGO and LSAG as soon as it is known that the Government may be supporting a PMB to ensure the LIM can be cleared. Contact UDD to discuss any devolution implications. Seek King's and/or Prince's consent, if applicable. Ask the Member if OPC can hand in the bill and supporting documents. If the Government wishes to make amendments to the bill, be clear in the write round what those amendments are. If the Minister wishes to oppose the bill, write to PBL Committee for information ahead of Second Reading setting out the reasons why.

Government supported PMBs - after introduction

Support the Member as necessary (in consultation with the Whips offices). Provide the minister with a briefing pack for all stages of the bill in the first House. Ensure the impact assessment and delegated powers memo have been published and the latter shared with the Commons PBO for publication(requires sending to the DPRRC when the Bill reaches the Lords - see Lords intro requirements). Ensure collective agreement is gained for any further amendments. If any amendments are made to the bill, ensure the explanatory notes are updated to reflect the changes. If necessary, write to the devolved governments asking that they initiate the legislative consent process. Ensure you have a good understanding of whether the relevant devolved governments are likely to secure a legislative consent motion before Report Stage in the first House. Work with the Member to identify a MP/peer to pick up the bill in the second House.

Consider and set up systems to support the Member in the second House (in consultation with the Whips offices). Send the delegated powers memorandum to the DPRRC Committee (cc’d to the PBO) when the bill passes to the second House. Provide the minister with a briefing pack for all stages of the bill in the second House. Arrange for a Minister, who is a privy counsellor, to signify Consent just before Third Reading, if applicable. If the bill reaches Royal Assent, send the final explanatory notes to Legislation Services at The National Archives for formatting and publication alongside the text of the Act.

Government handout bills

Bid for the bill, completing a bid template provided by the PBL Secretariat. Obtain drafting authority from the Leader of the House of Commons for OPC to draft the bill. Obtain policy clearance for the content of the hand out bill ahead of the ballot. Draft a ‘handout one pager’ setting out what the Bill does and share with the PBL Secretariat. Agree a timetable to provide OPC with drafting instructions and provide them on time. Ahead of the write round, engage with the PBL Secretariat and ask for the bill templates. Attach to the write round: PBL memorandum Bill print Explanatory notes Legal issues memorandum (LIM) Parliamentary handling strategy, Delegated powers memo (if applicable) Impact assessment (if applicable). Contact the AGO and LSAG as soon as it is known that the Government may be supporting a PMB to ensure the LIM can be cleared. Contact UDD to discuss any devolution implications. Seek King's and/or Prince's consent, if applicable.

Ask the Member if OPC can hand in the bill and supporting documents. If a Member picks up the bill, consider and set up systems to support the Member (in consultation with the Whips offices). Provide the minister with a briefing pack for all stages of the bill in the first House. Ensure the impact assessment and delegated powers memo have been published and the latter shared with the DPRRC. Ensure collective agreement is gained for any further amendments. If any amendments are made to the bill, ensure the explanatory notes are updated to reflect the changes. Ensure you have a good understanding of whether the relevant devolved governments are likely to secure a legislative consent motion before Report Stage in the first House. Work with the Member to identify a MP/peer to pick up the bill in the second House. Consider and set up systems to support the Member in the second House (in consultation with the Whips offices). Send the delegated powers memorandum to the DPRRC Committee (cc’d to the PBO) when the bill passes to the second House. Provide the minister with a briefing pack for all stages of the bill in the second House. If the bill reaches Royal Assent, send the final explanatory notes to Legislation Services at The National Archives for formatting and publication alongside the text of the Act.
